<a id='question-136'></a>

### Question 136: Anterior Urethral Rupture
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical boundaries of the male perineum and the potential spread of extravasated fluid (urine/blood) following an anterior urethral rupture, specifically the limitations imposed by fascial planes like Colles' fascia and Scarpa's fascia. (理解男性会阴的解剖边界以及前尿道破裂后尿液/血液外渗的潜在扩散，特别是科尔斯筋膜和斯卡帕筋膜等筋膜平面施加的限制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 28-year-old male presents to the emergency department after sustaining a straddle injury while riding a bicycle. He complains of severe perineal pain, inability to void, and blood at the urethral meatus. Physical examination reveals significant bruising and swelling of the scrotum, penis, and lower abdominal wall extending down to the perineum. A Foley catheter cannot be passed. Suprapubic catheterization is successfully performed, yielding grossly bloody urine. Retrograde urethrogram reveals a complete disruption of the urethra at the level of the bulbomembranous junction, with extravasation of contrast into the surrounding tissues. Given the clinical presentation and imaging findings, where is the extravasated urine and blood MOST likely limited in its spread within the anterior perineal space?

(A) Extending superiorly into the abdominal cavity through the deep inguinal ring.
(B) Limited inferiorly by the perineal membrane and superiorly by the external urethral sphincter.
(C) Confined within the superficial perineal pouch, limited superiorly by Colles' fascia and inferiorly by the perineal membrane.
(D) Spreading freely throughout the retroperitoneal space due to rupture of the prostatic capsule.
(E) Limited laterally by the ischiocavernosus muscle and medially by the bulbospongiosus muscle, but extending superiorly into the scrotum.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Straddle injury:** Mechanism suggestive of urethral trauma, particularly at the bulbomembranous junction.
- **Inability to void, blood at meatus:** Classic signs of urethral injury.
- **Bruising/swelling of scrotum, penis, lower abdominal wall:** Indicates extravasation of urine and blood into the surrounding tissues.
- **Foley catheter failure, successful suprapubic catheter:** Confirms urethral disruption distal to the bladder neck.
- **Retrograde urethrogram:** Confirms urethral rupture at the bulbomembranous junction.
- **Extravasation of contrast:** Visual confirmation of fluid leaking into surrounding tissues.
- **Question:** Asks for the anatomical limits of this extravasation.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Anterior Urethral Rupture:** Typically occurs at the bulbomembranous junction (where the corpus spongiosum surrounds the urethra) due to straddle injuries.
- **Extravasation Pathway:** Urine and blood leak into the surrounding tissues. The key is understanding the fascial boundaries of the male perineum.
- **Superficial Perineal Pouch:** This space is located between the Colles' fascia (superficial perineal fascia) superiorly and the perineal membrane inferiorly. It contains the root of the penis (corpus cavernosum, corpus spongiosum, urethra), the bulbospongiosus muscle, the ischiocavernosus muscle, and the superficial transverse perineal muscles.
- **Colles' Fascia:** A layer of deep fascia continuous with Scarpa's fascia of the anterior abdominal wall superiorly and the dartos fascia of the scrotum inferiorly. It forms the superior boundary of the superficial perineal pouch.
- **Perineal Membrane:** A strong fibrous sheet that forms the inferior boundary of the superficial perineal pouch and the superior boundary of the deep perineal pouch.
- **Scarpa's Fascia:** Deep fascia of the anterior abdominal wall, continuous with Colles' fascia. This limits superior spread into the abdominal wall.
- **Deep Perineal Pouch:** Located inferior to the perineal membrane, contains the external urethral sphincter, deep transverse perineal muscles, and neurovascular structures. Extravasation from an *anterior* urethral rupture primarily stays within the superficial pouch.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The bruising and swelling described (scrotum, penis, lower abdominal wall) are consistent with extravasation within the superficial perineal pouch, limited superiorly by Colles' fascia (which is continuous with Scarpa's fascia limiting spread into the abdominal wall) and inferiorly by the perineal membrane.

##### 3. Embryological Considerations (胚胎发生学考点)
- The perineal structures develop from the urogenital sinus and the cloaca. The perineal membrane forms from the fusion of the urorectal septum. Colles' fascia is derived from the fascia lata. Understanding these origins helps visualize the fascial continuity.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** Extravasation is limited superiorly by Colles' fascia, which is continuous with Scarpa's fascia, preventing free spread into the abdominal cavity through the deep inguinal ring. The deep inguinal ring is located lateral to the superficial perineal pouch.
- (B): **Incorrect.** While the external urethral sphincter is superior to the rupture site, the primary superior limit of extravasation in the superficial pouch is Colles' fascia. The perineal membrane is the inferior limit, not a superior limit.
- (D): **Incorrect.** This describes a posterior urethral rupture (often associated with pelvic fractures), where extravasation can occur into the retroperitoneal space. Anterior urethral ruptures are contained within the perineal pouches. The prostatic capsule is intact in this scenario.
- (E): **Incorrect.** The ischiocavernosus and bulbospongiosus muscles are within the superficial perineal pouch, but they define the lateral and medial boundaries, not the limits of extravasation. Extravasation *does* extend into the scrotum (due to continuity of Colles'/Dartos fascia) and penis, but the superior limit is Colles' fascia, not the scrotum itself.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature                 | Superficial Perineal Pouch                                  | Deep Perineal Pouch                                      | Retroperitoneal Space                                  | Abdominal Cavity                                     |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------- |
| **Boundaries**          | Superior: Colles' fascia; Inferior: Perineal membrane       | Superior: Perineal membrane; Inferior: Pelvic diaphragm | Superior: Pelvic diaphragm; Inferior: Obturator internus | Superior: Transversalis fascia; Inferior: Peritoneum |
| **Contents**            | Root of penis, bulbospongiosus, ischiocavernosus, urethra | External urethral sphincter, deep transverse perineal ms. | Bladder, rectum, vessels, nerves                      | Viscera, peritoneum                                   |
| **Extravasation Limit** | Colles' fascia (superior), Perineal membrane (inferior)     | Perineal membrane (superior)                             | Pelvic diaphragm                                       | Transversalis fascia                                  |
| **Relevant Fascia**     | Colles' fascia (continuous with Scarpa's fascia)            | Perineal membrane                                         | N/A                                                   | Transversalis fascia                                  |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Anterior urethral ruptures typically result in extravasation limited by the fascial boundaries of the superficial perineal pouch (Colles' fascia superiorly, perineal membrane inferiorly). This contrasts with posterior urethral ruptures, which can involve the retroperitoneal space. Remember the continuity of Colles' fascia with Scarpa's fascia and Dartos fascia, explaining spread to the scrotum and lower abdominal wall. (前尿道破裂导致的外渗受男性会阴浅层筋膜囊的筋膜边界限制，即上方为科尔斯筋膜，下方为会阴膜。这与后尿道破裂不同，后者可能累及腹膜后间隙。记住科尔斯筋膜与斯卡帕筋膜和阴囊的达尔托斯筋膜的连续性，这解释了外渗扩散到阴囊和下腹壁的原因。)

<a id='question-137'></a>

### Question 137: Uterus Anatomy & Ligaments: Broad Ligament (Mesometrium, Mesovarium, Mesosalpinx)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The broad ligament (mesometrium) is a double layer of peritoneum extending from the sides of the uterus to the lateral walls and floor of the pelvis. It contains the uterine tubes (fallopian tubes), ovaries, round ligaments, ovarian ligaments, and uterine vessels. Understanding its subdivisions (mesosalpinx, mesovarium, mesometrium) and contents is crucial for localizing pathology. (阔韧带是子宫两侧延伸至盆腔侧壁和底部的双层腹膜，包含输卵管、卵巢、圆韧带、卵巢韧带和子宫血管。理解其亚部（输卵管系膜、卵巢系膜、子宫系膜）和内容物对于定位病理至关重要。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old female presents to the emergency department with acute onset, severe left lower quadrant pain, nausea, and vaginal bleeding. Her last menstrual period was 6 weeks ago. She reports a history of pelvic inflammatory disease (PID) 2 years prior. On physical examination, she is febrile (38.5°C) and tachycardic (110 bpm). Abdominal examination reveals significant tenderness and guarding in the left lower quadrant, with rebound tenderness. A pelvic ultrasound reveals a complex, heterogeneous mass adjacent to the left uterine horn, measuring approximately 5 cm in diameter, with surrounding free fluid. The mass appears to be located within the broad ligament, superior to the left ovary. Given the clinical presentation and imaging findings, which structure is most likely involved?

(A) Mesovarium
(B) Mesosalpinx
(C) Mesometrium
(D) Round ligament of the uterus
(E) Uterosacral ligament

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old female, reproductive age.
- **Symptoms:** Acute severe left lower quadrant pain, nausea, vaginal bleeding, fever, tachycardia. Suggests an acute inflammatory or infectious process, possibly involving the reproductive organs.
- **History:** Prior PID increases the risk of tubo-ovarian abscess (TOA) or ectopic pregnancy.
- **Physical Exam:** Fever, tachycardia, left lower quadrant tenderness, guarding, rebound tenderness. Consistent with peritonitis or significant pelvic inflammation/infection.
- **Ultrasound Findings:** Complex, heterogeneous mass (5 cm) adjacent to the left uterine horn, within the broad ligament, superior to the left ovary, with surrounding free fluid. This strongly suggests an inflammatory mass, likely a TOA, located within the broad ligament near the uterine tube.
- **Question Stem:** Asks to identify the specific structure within the broad ligament where the mass is located.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Broad Ligament (Mesometrium):** A double fold of peritoneum extending from the lateral pelvic walls to the uterus. It encloses the uterus, uterine tubes, ovaries, round ligaments, and associated vessels. It is divided into three parts:
    - **Mesosalpinx:** The superior portion that suspends the uterine tube (fallopian tube). It contains the uterine tube, its vessels, and nerves.
    - **Mesovarium:** The portion that suspends the ovary. It contains the ovary, its vessels, and nerves.
    - **Mesometrium:** The largest, inferior portion that covers the body and cervix of the uterus. It contains the uterus, uterine vessels, nerves, and lymphatics.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's history of PID, acute symptoms, and ultrasound findings (mass adjacent to uterine horn, within broad ligament, superior to ovary) are highly suggestive of a tubo-ovarian abscess (TOA). TOAs typically form in the mesosalpinx, involving the uterine tube and adjacent ovary, often secondary to ascending infection from PID. The location described (adjacent to uterine horn, within broad ligament, superior to ovary) precisely fits the location of the uterine tube within the mesosalpinx.
- **Differential Diagnosis:**
    - Ectopic pregnancy: Can occur in the tube (salpingitis isthmicus) or elsewhere, but the complex mass with surrounding fluid and history of PID points more towards infection/abscess.
    - Ovarian cyst/torsion: Usually located within the mesovarium, not typically adjacent to the uterine horn.
    - Uterine fibroid: Located within the uterine wall (myometrium) or attached to the uterine surface (subserosal), not typically within the broad ligament itself.
    - Appendicitis: Pain usually starts periumbilical and migrates to the right lower quadrant.
- **Conclusion:** The clinical picture and imaging findings strongly localize the pathology to the uterine tube within the mesosalpinx, a common site for TOA formation, especially in patients with a history of PID.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Mesovarium:** This part of the broad ligament suspends the ovary. While an ovarian abscess can occur, the mass is described as adjacent to the *uterine horn* and *superior* to the ovary, making the mesovarium less likely as the primary location. Ovarian pathology is usually centered around the ovary itself.
- (C) **Mesometrium:** This is the main part of the broad ligament covering the uterus. Pathology here would typically involve the uterine wall itself (e.g., fibroids, adenomyosis) or structures directly attached to the uterus, not a mass specifically adjacent to the uterine horn and superior to the ovary within the broad ligament.
- (D) **Round ligament of the uterus:** This ligament extends from the uterine horn, through the inguinal canal, to the labia majora. It is located *within* the broad ligament but is a distinct structure. Pathology involving the round ligament (e.g., round ligament pregnancy, torsion) would present differently and wouldn't typically form a complex mass adjacent to the uterine horn *within* the broad ligament in this manner.
- (E) **Uterosacral ligament:** These ligaments extend from the cervix and upper vagina to the sacrum. They are posterior supports of the uterus and are not part of the broad ligament itself. Pathology involving these ligaments is rare and would present differently.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure        | Location within Broad Ligament | Contents                                     | Typical Pathology                               | Relation to Uterine Horn/Ovary |
|-----------------|---------------------------------|----------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------|
| Mesosalpinx     | Superior part                   | Uterine tube, vessels, nerves                 | Tubo-ovarian abscess (TOA), ectopic pregnancy   | Adjacent to uterine horn, superior to ovary |
| Mesovarium      | Middle part                     | Ovary, vessels, nerves                       | Ovarian cyst, ovarian torsion, ovarian cancer | Adjacent to ovary             |
| Mesometrium     | Inferior part                   | Uterus, uterine vessels, nerves, lymphatics | Uterine fibroids, adenomyosis                  | Covers uterus                 |
| Round Ligament  | Within broad ligament           | Fibromuscular cord                           | Round ligament pregnancy, torsion              | Extends from uterine horn     |
| Uterosacral Ligament | Posterior support, not in broad ligament | Fibromuscular cord                           | N/A                                             | Attaches to cervix/sacrum     |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The broad ligament is divided into mesosalpinx, mesovarium, and mesometrium. Pathology involving the uterine tube (e.g., TOA, ectopic pregnancy) typically occurs within the mesosalpinx, located adjacent to the uterine horn and superior to the ovary. Differentiating these subdivisions is crucial for localizing pelvic pathology based on clinical presentation and imaging. Key differentials include ovarian pathology (mesovarium), uterine pathology (mesometrium), and other pelvic structures like the round ligament or uterosacral ligaments.

<a id='question-138'></a>

### Question 138: Round Ligament of Uterus: Gubernaculum Derivative, Traverses Inguinal Canal to Labium Majus
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The round ligament of the uterus is a derivative of the gubernaculum and its anatomical course through the inguinal canal, its attachment points, and potential clinical implications (e.g., pain, masses). (子宫圆韧带是 gubernaculum 的衍生物，其通过腹股沟管的解剖路径、附着点及其潜在的临床意义（例如疼痛、肿块）。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old female presents to the emergency department complaining of severe, intermittent right lower quadrant pain that started 3 days ago. The pain is described as sharp and colicky, radiating to her right groin and labia majora. She denies fever, chills, vaginal discharge, or urinary symptoms. Her last menstrual period was 6 weeks ago, and she has a history of one previous uncomplicated vaginal delivery. Physical examination reveals significant tenderness upon palpation of the right lower quadrant and right inguinal region. A palpable, firm, sausage-shaped mass is noted extending from the right inguinal canal towards the labia majora. Ultrasound confirms a thickened, edematous structure extending from the uterine fundus, traversing the inguinal canal, and terminating near the labia majora. The structure shows increased vascularity on Doppler imaging. Based on the anatomical course and clinical presentation, which of the following structures is most likely involved?

(A) Fallopian tube
(B) Ovarian ligament
(C) Round ligament of the uterus
(D) Broad ligament
(E) Uterosacral ligament

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old female, history of pregnancy/delivery.
- **Chief Complaint:** Severe, intermittent right lower quadrant pain radiating to groin/labia majora.
- **Physical Exam:** Tenderness in RLQ and right inguinal region, palpable firm mass extending from inguinal canal to labia majora.
- **Imaging:** Ultrasound shows thickened, edematous structure from uterine fundus, through inguinal canal, terminating near labia majora, with increased vascularity.
- **Key Clues:** The location of pain (RLQ radiating to groin/labia), the palpable mass extending *through the inguinal canal* towards the labia majora, and the ultrasound findings pinpointing a structure originating from the uterus, traversing the inguinal canal, and ending near the labia majora. This specific anatomical course is characteristic of the round ligament of the uterus. The increased vascularity suggests inflammation or torsion.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Round Ligament of the Uterus:** This structure is a remnant of the gubernaculum, an embryological structure that guides the descent of the gonads. In females, the gubernaculum gives rise to the round ligament of the uterus and the ovarian ligament.
- **Origin:** Arises from the uterine horns, just lateral to the fallopian tubes, near the junction of the uterus and fallopian tube.
- **Course:** Passes laterally through the pelvic wall, enters the deep inguinal ring, traverses the inguinal canal (lateral to the spermatic cord in females), exits through the superficial inguinal ring, and inserts into the labia majora.
- **Function:** Supports the uterus, maintaining its anteverted position.
- **Clinical Relevance:** Inflammation (oophoritis), torsion, or hematoma of the round ligament can cause lower abdominal or inguinal pain, often mimicking appendicitis or other gynecological emergencies. A palpable mass may be present along its course. The described clinical presentation and imaging findings are highly suggestive of round ligament pathology.
- **Embryology:** Derived from the caudal gubernaculum. The gubernaculum connects the developing gonads to the labioscrotal swellings (females) or genital tubercle (males). In females, the cranial part becomes the ovarian ligament, and the caudal part becomes the round ligament of the uterus.
- **Comparison to other structures:**
    - Fallopian tube: Located within the broad ligament, does not traverse the inguinal canal. Ectopic pregnancy can cause pain, but the mass location is different.
    - Ovarian ligament: Connects the ovary to the uterus, does not traverse the inguinal canal.
    - Broad ligament: A double layer of peritoneum extending from the sides of the uterus to the pelvic walls, containing the fallopian tubes, ovaries, round ligaments, and uterine vessels. It does not have a discrete course through the inguinal canal to the labia majora.
    - Uterosacral ligament: Connects the cervix and upper vagina to the sacrum, providing posterior support to the uterus. Not related to the inguinal canal or labia majora.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Fallopian tube: The fallopian tube is located within the broad ligament, medial to the ovary. It does not traverse the inguinal canal. While tubal pathology (like ectopic pregnancy or salpingitis) can cause lower abdominal pain, the palpable mass extending *through the inguinal canal* to the labia majora is inconsistent with fallopian tube involvement.
- (B) Ovarian ligament: The ovarian ligament connects the ovary to the uterus, just below the entry point of the fallopian tube. It is located within the broad ligament and does not pass through the inguinal canal. Its pathology would typically present with ovarian or pelvic pain, not a mass extending to the labia majora via the inguinal canal.
- (D) Broad ligament: The broad ligament is a large peritoneal fold that drapes over the uterus, fallopian tubes, and ovaries. While the round ligament is *contained within* the broad ligament in its pelvic portion, the broad ligament itself does not have the specific course described (through the inguinal canal to the labia majora). Pathology of the broad ligament is less specific and wouldn't typically present as a discrete mass traversing the inguinal canal.
- (E) Uterosacral ligament: These ligaments connect the cervix posteriorly to the sacrum. They provide posterior support to the uterus and are located deep within the pelvis, far from the inguinal canal and labia majora. Pathology here would cause posterior pelvic pain or issues with uterine position, not the described presentation.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                 | Origin                | Course                                                                 | Termination           | Relation to Inguinal Canal | Relation to Labia Majora | Embryological Origin |
| :------------------------ | :-------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------- | :------------------------ | :---------------------- | :------------------- |
| Round Ligament of Uterus  | Uterine horns         | Pelvis -> Deep inguinal ring -> Inguinal canal -> Superficial ring -> Labia Majora | Labia Majora          | Traverses                | Yes                     | Gubernaculum (caudal) |
| Fallopian Tube            | Uterine horns         | Within broad ligament, towards ovary                                  | Ovary (fimbriated end) | No                       | No                      | Mesonephric duct     |
| Ovarian Ligament          | Uterus (near tube)    | Within broad ligament, to ovary                                       | Ovary                 | No                       | No                      | Gubernaculum (cranial)|
| Broad Ligament            | Pelvic sidewalls      | Drapes over uterus, tubes, ovaries                                     | Pelvic walls          | Contains round ligament   | No                      | Peritoneum          |
| Uterosacral Ligament      | Cervix/Upper Vagina   | To sacrum (posteriorly)                                                | Sacrum                | No                       | No                      | Mesovarium/Mesometrium|

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The round ligament of the uterus is a gubernaculum derivative that uniquely traverses the inguinal canal to insert into the labia majora. Its specific anatomical course makes it susceptible to pathology presenting as lower abdominal/inguinal pain and a palpable mass extending through the canal. Differentiating it from other pelvic structures (fallopian tube, ovarian ligament, broad ligament, uterosacral ligament) requires knowledge of their origins, courses, and terminations. Key differentials for lower abdominal/inguinal pain in females include appendicitis, ectopic pregnancy, ovarian torsion, and round ligament pathology.

<a id='question-139'></a>

### Question 139: Pelvic Anatomy & Surgical Risk
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The cardinal ligament (transverse cervical ligament) is a major supporting structure of the uterus and vagina, containing the uterine artery and vein, and the ureter passes inferiorly near it. Injury to this ligament or its contents during pelvic surgery (e.g., hysterectomy) can lead to significant hemorrhage or ureteral damage. (子宫主韧带/基韧带是子宫和阴道的主要支撑结构，包含子宫动脉和静脉，输尿管在其下方经过。在盆腔手术（如子宫切除术）中损伤该韧带或其内容物可能导致严重出血或输尿管损伤。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old female (G4P4) presents to the gynecological oncology clinic for evaluation of abnormal uterine bleeding for the past 6 months. Her history includes hypertension and type 2 diabetes mellitus. A transvaginal ultrasound reveals a thickened endometrium, and an endometrial biopsy confirms endometrial adenocarcinoma, FIGO stage IA. She is scheduled for a total abdominal hysterectomy with bilateral salpingo-oophorectomy. During the procedure, the surgeon identifies the ureters entering the bladder and proceeds with the dissection. While ligating the uterine vessels near the cervix, there is sudden, profuse bleeding. The surgeon immediately clamps the vessels proximal to the bleeding site. Postoperatively, the patient develops hematuria, and a cystogram reveals extravasation of contrast near the trigone.

Which structure is MOST likely involved in both the intraoperative hemorrhage and the postoperative hematuria?

(A) Ovarian ligament
(B) Round ligament of the uterus
(C) Cardinal ligament (Transverse cervical ligament)
(D) Uterosacral ligament
(E) Broad ligament

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 52-year-old female with abnormal uterine bleeding, diagnosed with endometrial adenocarcinoma. This sets the stage for a hysterectomy.
- **Procedure:** Total abdominal hysterectomy with bilateral salpingo-oophorectomy. This involves extensive pelvic dissection.
- **Intraoperative Event:** Profuse bleeding occurs while ligating uterine vessels near the cervix. This points to injury of a major vessel supplying the uterus in that region.
- **Postoperative Complication:** Hematuria with cystogram showing contrast extravasation near the trigone. This indicates injury to the urinary tract, specifically the bladder or ureter near the bladder.
- **Connecting the Events:** The bleeding occurred during ligation of vessels near the cervix, and the subsequent hematuria suggests injury to the ureter, which runs close to the uterine vessels in this region. The structure containing both the uterine artery (source of bleeding) and closely related to the ureter (cause of hematuria) is the cardinal ligament.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Cardinal Ligament (Transverse Cervical Ligament / Mackenrodt Ligament):** This is a major supporting structure of the uterus and cervix, extending from the lateral pelvic wall to the cervix and upper vagina. It is composed of dense connective tissue.
- **Contents:** Crucially, the cardinal ligament contains the uterine artery and vein, which run medially within it to reach the uterus. The ureter passes inferiorly and laterally, running *under* the uterine artery near the cervix, approximately 1-2 cm lateral to the internal cervical os.
- **Clinical Relevance:** During hysterectomy, the cardinal ligaments are clamped, cut, and ligated to detach the uterus from the pelvic sidewall. Injury to the uterine artery within the ligament causes significant hemorrhage. Injury to the ureter, which lies close to the uterine artery within or adjacent to the cardinal ligament, can occur during this dissection, leading to postoperative hematuria, ureteral stricture, or fistula. The location of the bleeding (near the cervix during vessel ligation) and the subsequent ureteral injury (hematuria near the trigone) strongly implicate the cardinal ligament.
- **Embryology:** The cardinal ligament develops from the gubernaculum and the pelvic fascia.
- **Comparison to other ligaments:**
    - **Ovarian ligament:** Connects the ovary to the uterus; does not contain the uterine artery or ureter.
    - **Round ligament:** Connects the uterus to the labia majora; passes through the inguinal canal; does not contain the uterine artery or ureter near the cervix.
    - **Uterosacral ligament:** Connects the cervix to the sacrum; provides posterior support; does not contain the uterine artery or ureter in the same close proximity as the cardinal ligament.
    - **Broad ligament:** A double layer of peritoneum extending from the lateral pelvic walls to the uterus; contains the fallopian tubes, round ligaments, ovarian ligaments, uterine vessels, and ureters, but the cardinal ligament is a distinct, dense connective tissue structure within the lower part of the broad ligament, specifically responsible for lateral support and containing the key vessels and ureter at risk during hysterectomy.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Ovarian ligament:** Connects the ovary to the uterus. It does not contain the uterine artery or run in close proximity to the ureter near the cervix. Injury would not explain the intraoperative hemorrhage from the uterine vessel or the subsequent ureteral injury.
- (B) **Round ligament of the uterus:** Connects the uterine fundus to the labia majora via the inguinal canal. It does not contain the uterine artery or run near the ureter at the level of the cervix. Injury is unlikely during hysterectomy dissection near the cervix and would not cause these specific complications.
- (D) **Uterosacral ligament:** Provides posterior support to the uterus and cervix, connecting them to the sacrum. While the ureter runs near the uterosacral ligament posteriorly, the uterine artery is not contained within it. Injury to this ligament is less likely to cause the described profuse bleeding from a uterine vessel during cervical dissection.
- (E) **Broad ligament:** This is a large peritoneal fold containing many structures, including the uterine vessels, ureters, fallopian tubes, and round/ovarian ligaments. While the cardinal ligament is located within the lower part of the broad ligament, the broad ligament itself is a general term. The specific structure containing the uterine artery and closely related to the ureter at the site of injury is the cardinal ligament. Choosing the broad ligament is too general and doesn't pinpoint the specific anatomical structure responsible for both the hemorrhage and the ureteral injury in this context.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure             | Contents                                  | Relation to Ureter                                  | Relation to Uterine Artery                               | Clinical Relevance (Hysterectomy)                                  |
|----------------------|-------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Cardinal Ligament** | Uterine artery, vein, nerves, connective tissue | Runs inferiorly, lateral to artery near cervix (~1-2 cm) | Runs medially within the ligament                          | **High risk for hemorrhage (artery injury) and ureteral injury.** |
| Ovarian Ligament     | Ovarian vessels, nerves                    | Not closely related near cervix                      | Not contained                                             | Injury during oophorectomy, not relevant to cervical dissection. |
| Round Ligament       | Nerves, vessels (small)                    | Not closely related near cervix                      | Not contained                                             | Injury during ligation at fundus, not relevant to cervical dissection. |
| Uterosacral Ligament | Nerves, vessels (small)                    | Runs posteriorly, close relation                      | Not contained                                             | Provides posterior support, less likely site for major vessel injury. |
| Broad Ligament       | Uterus, tubes, ovaries, vessels, ureters, etc. | Contains ureter                                     | Contains uterine vessels                                  | General structure; cardinal ligament is the specific structure at risk. |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The cardinal ligament (transverse cervical ligament) is a critical structure containing the uterine artery and closely related to the ureter near the cervix. Injury during hysterectomy can cause significant hemorrhage (uterine artery) and ureteral damage (hematuria). Differentiating the cardinal ligament from other pelvic ligaments based on contents and spatial relationships is crucial for understanding surgical risks. Key differentials include the ovarian, round, and uterosacral ligaments, which have different contents and locations.

---

<a id='question-140'></a>

### Question 140: Uterosacral Ligament: Suspends Cervix from Sacrum, Infiltration in Endometriosis
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The uterosacral ligaments are crucial pelvic supports, extending from the cervix/upper vagina to the sacrum. Endometriosis commonly affects these ligaments, causing pain and nodularity, particularly in the rectovaginal septum. Understanding their anatomical course and function is key to diagnosing and managing endometriosis. (子宫骶韧带是重要的盆腔支持结构，从宫颈/上阴道延伸至骶骨。子宫内膜异位症常累及该韧带，导致疼痛和结节，尤其是在直肠阴道隔。了解其解剖走行和功能对于诊断和管理子宫内膜异位症至关重要。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 38-year-old woman presents to the gynecology clinic complaining of severe dysmenorrhea, dyspareunia (painful intercourse), and chronic pelvic pain, worsening during menstruation. She has been trying to conceive for 2 years without success. Her past medical history is significant for stage III endometriosis diagnosed via laparoscopy 5 years ago, treated with hormonal therapy and GnRH agonists, which provided temporary relief. Physical examination reveals significant tenderness upon palpation of the posterior vaginal fornix and the rectovaginal septum. A speculum examination shows a bluish, nodular thickening in the posterior cul-de-sac, extending towards the rectum. Transvaginal ultrasound shows multiple hypoechoic nodules within the pelvic cavity, particularly along the posterior aspect of the uterus and rectum. A subsequent MRI confirms the presence of deep infiltrating endometriosis involving the posterior pelvic wall, specifically demonstrating nodules within the tissue planes between the uterus/cervix and the rectum, consistent with involvement of the uterosacral ligaments. Given the patient's symptoms and imaging findings, which anatomical structure is most likely infiltrated by the endometriotic lesions causing her symptoms?

(A) Cardinal ligament (Transverse cervical ligament)
(B) Round ligament of the uterus
(C) Uterosacral ligament
(D) Broad ligament
(E) Pubocervical ligament

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 38-year-old woman with severe dysmenorrhea, dyspareunia, chronic pelvic pain, infertility, and history of stage III endometriosis. This points towards a significant pelvic pathology, likely recurrent or persistent endometriosis.
- **Physical Examination:** Tenderness in the posterior vaginal fornix and rectovaginal septum, bluish nodular thickening in the posterior cul-de-sac extending towards the rectum. This strongly suggests deep infiltrating endometriosis affecting the posterior pelvic structures.
- **Imaging Findings:** Transvaginal ultrasound and MRI confirm deep infiltrating endometriosis involving the posterior pelvic wall, specifically nodules between the uterus/cervix and the rectum. This localization is highly characteristic of uterosacral ligament involvement.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the specific anatomical structure infiltrated by the endometriotic lesions.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Uterosacral Ligaments (子宫骶韧带):** These are paired ligaments that provide crucial support to the uterus and cervix, preventing prolapse. They originate from the lateral aspects of the cervix and the upper vagina, extending posteriorly and superiorly to attach to the anterior surface of the sacrum (specifically, the sacral promontory and the sacrotuberous ligaments). They pass through the posterior cul-de-sac, lateral to the rectum.
- **Endometriosis (子宫内膜异位症):** A condition where endometrial-like tissue grows outside the uterus. Common sites include the ovaries, fallopian tubes, pelvic peritoneum, and rectovaginal septum. Deep infiltrating endometriosis (DIE) specifically involves invasion of structures beneath the peritoneum, such as the bowel, bladder, ureters, and uterosacral ligaments.
- **Clinical Correlation:** DIE involving the uterosacral ligaments is a frequent cause of severe dysmenorrhea, dyspareunia (especially deep dyspareunia), chronic pelvic pain, and infertility. The nodules formed by endometriosis in these ligaments can cause significant tenderness and pain, particularly during menstruation or intercourse. The location described in the vignette (posterior cul-de-sac, extending towards the rectum, between uterus/cervix and rectum) is classic for uterosacral ligament involvement.
- **Embryology:** The uterosacral ligaments are derived from the mesoderm of the pelvic wall and the connective tissue of the developing Müllerian system (paramesonephric ducts).
- **Differential Diagnosis:** The symptoms and location described are highly specific for uterosacral ligament endometriosis. Other structures can be involved, but the posterior location and connection to both the cervix/uterus and the sacrum/rectum point strongly to the uterosacral ligaments.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Cardinal ligament (Transverse cervical ligament):** These ligaments are the primary support structures for the cervix and uterus, extending from the cervix and upper vagina laterally to the pelvic sidewalls. While endometriosis can affect the cardinal ligaments, they are located more laterally than the uterosacral ligaments and are less commonly involved in deep infiltrating endometriosis causing posterior pelvic pain and rectovaginal symptoms. The vignette specifically describes posterior findings.
- (B) **Round ligament of the uterus:** These ligaments extend from the uterine horns, pass through the inguinal canal, and attach to the labia majora. They provide anteverted support to the uterus. Endometriosis involving the round ligaments typically presents as a palpable, tender mass in the inguinal region or groin, not posterior pelvic pain or rectovaginal symptoms.
- (D) **Broad ligament:** This is a double layer of peritoneum that drapes over the uterus, fallopian tubes, and ovaries, extending laterally to the pelvic sidewalls. It contains the uterine artery, ovarian artery, nerves, and lymphatics. While endometriosis can occur within the broad ligament (superficial or deep), the specific description of nodules between the cervix/uterus and the rectum, causing posterior pain and rectovaginal symptoms, is much more characteristic of uterosacral ligament involvement. The broad ligament is a more general structure.
- (E) **Pubocervical ligament:** These ligaments extend from the cervix anteriorly and inferiorly to the pubic bone. They provide anterior support to the cervix. Endometriosis involving these ligaments is rare and would typically cause anterior pelvic pain or urinary symptoms, not the posterior symptoms described in the vignette.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure             | Origin                                  | Insertion                               | Location                                      | Primary Function                      | Common Endometriosis Symptoms/Signs                                  |
|----------------------|-----------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Uterosacral Ligament** | Cervix/Upper Vagina                     | Sacrum (promontory, sacrotuberous lig.) | Posterior pelvic cavity, between uterus/rectum | Posterior uterine/cervical support     | **Posterior pelvic pain, deep dyspareunia, rectovaginal nodule/tenderness** |
| Cardinal Ligament    | Cervix/Upper Vagina                     | Pelvic Sidewall                           | Lateral pelvic cavity                         | Lateral uterine/cervical support       | Lateral pelvic pain (less common for DIE)                            |
| Round Ligament        | Uterine Horns                            | Labia Majora                              | Inguinal Canal, Anterior Pelvis                | Anteverted uterine support             | Inguinal mass/pain                                                    |
| Broad Ligament        | Peritoneum draping uterus/adnexa        | Pelvic Sidewall                           | Surrounds uterus/adnexa                       | Encloses vessels, nerves, lymphatics | Variable, depends on specific location within ligament                |
| Pubocervical Ligament | Cervix                                  | Pubic Bone                                | Anterior pelvic cavity                        | Anterior cervical support              | Anterior pelvic pain (rare for DIE)                                  |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Deep infiltrating endometriosis frequently involves the uterosacral ligaments, causing characteristic posterior pelvic pain, deep dyspareunia, and rectovaginal nodularity. Differentiating uterosacral ligament involvement from other pelvic structures (cardinal, round, broad, pubocervical ligaments) requires careful localization of symptoms and imaging findings. Remember the posterior location and connection to the cervix/uterus and rectum as key features for uterosacral ligament endometriosis.

<a id='question-141'></a>

### Question 141: Pelvic Anatomy & Vascular Supply
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Identify the structure containing the ovarian vessels within the broad ligament of the uterus, and understand its relationship to the pelvic sidewall and potential injury during pelvic surgery. (识别子宫阔韧带内包含卵巢血管的结构，以及它与盆腔侧壁的关系和盆腔手术中的潜在损伤。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 42-year-old female undergoes a laparoscopic right salpingo-oophorectomy for a suspected ovarian cyst. During the procedure, the surgeon identifies the infundibulopelvic ligament. While ligating the vessels within this structure, there is significant bleeding. Intraoperative ultrasound reveals a small, aberrant vessel arising from the external iliac artery and coursing superiorly alongside the ureter before joining the main ovarian vessels within the infundibulopelvic ligament. Postoperatively, the patient develops right lower extremity edema. Which of the following structures is MOST likely involved in the aberrant vascular supply and subsequent edema?

(A) Round ligament of the uterus
(B) Uterosacral ligament
(C) Cardinal ligament (Transverse cervical ligament)
(D) Suspensory ligament of the ovary (Infundibulopelvic ligament)
(E) Pubovesical ligament

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 42-year-old female undergoing right salpingo-oophorectomy. This sets the context for pelvic surgery involving the ovary and fallopian tube.
- **Procedure:** Laparoscopic right salpingo-oophorectomy. The surgical focus is on the right ovary and fallopian tube.
- **Anatomical Landmark:** Identification of the infundibulopelvic ligament. This is a key structure connecting the ovary to the pelvic sidewall.
- **Surgical Event:** Bleeding during ligation of vessels within the infundibulopelvic ligament. This indicates the presence of major vessels in this structure.
- **Imaging Finding:** Intraoperative ultrasound shows an aberrant vessel arising from the external iliac artery, coursing alongside the ureter, and joining the main ovarian vessels within the infundibulopelvic ligament. This suggests an anatomical variation involving the ovarian blood supply.
- **Postoperative Symptom:** Right lower extremity edema. This points towards venous obstruction or reflux in the right lower limb.
- **Question Stem:** Asks which structure is MOST likely involved in the aberrant vascular supply and subsequent edema. The combination of the surgical site (ovary), the identified ligament (infundibulopelvic), the aberrant vessel origin (external iliac), and the postoperative symptom (lower limb edema) strongly implicates a specific structure.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Suspensory Ligament of the Ovary (Infundibulopelvic Ligament):** This is a fold of peritoneum extending from the lateral pelvic wall to the ovary. It is part of the broad ligament of the uterus. Crucially, it contains the ovarian artery (branch of the abdominal aorta) and the ovarian vein (draining into the inferior vena cava, or sometimes the left renal vein). The ovarian nerve plexus also travels within this ligament.
- **Aberrant Vessel:** The description of a vessel arising from the external iliac artery and joining the ovarian vessels within the infundibulopelvic ligament is an anatomical variation. While the primary supply is from the aorta (artery) and IVC (vein), variations exist.
- **Lower Extremity Edema:** The ovarian vein drains into the IVC. The right ovarian vein drains directly into the IVC at an acute angle. The left ovarian vein drains into the left renal vein, which then drains into the IVC. Compression of the right ovarian vein between the aorta and the superior mesenteric artery (nutcracker syndrome variant) or thrombosis can lead to right lower extremity edema. An aberrant vessel from the external iliac artery joining the ovarian vein within the infundibulopelvic ligament could potentially contribute to venous congestion or reflux, especially if it involves the vein itself or creates a shunt, leading to edema. The infundibulopelvic ligament is the structure housing these vessels.
- **Other Ligaments:**
    - **Round ligament:** Extends from the uterine horn to the labia majora; contains remnants of the processus vaginalis; does not contain major ovarian vessels.
    - **Uterosacral ligament:** Extends from the cervix/upper vagina to the sacrum; provides support to the uterus; does not contain ovarian vessels.
    - **Cardinal ligament:** Extends from the cervix/upper vagina to the lateral pelvic wall; contains the uterine artery and vein; does not contain ovarian vessels.
    - **Pubovesical ligament:** Extends from the bladder neck to the pubic symphysis; supports the bladder; does not contain ovarian vessels.
- **Conclusion:** The clinical scenario directly implicates the infundibulopelvic ligament due to its known content (ovarian vessels), the surgical context (oophorectomy), the identified aberrant vessel within it, and the resulting symptom (lower limb edema related to ovarian vein drainage).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Round ligament of the uterus:** Incorrect. This ligament connects the uterus to the labia majora and does not contain the ovarian vessels. It is primarily involved in uterine anteversion.
- (B) **Uterosacral ligament:** Incorrect. This ligament provides posterior support to the uterus and cervix, attaching them to the sacrum. It does not contain the ovarian vessels.
- (C) **Cardinal ligament (Transverse cervical ligament):** Incorrect. This ligament provides lateral support to the uterus and cervix, attaching them to the pelvic sidewall. It contains the uterine artery and vein, not the ovarian vessels. Injury here can cause massive hemorrhage during hysterectomy.
- (E) **Pubovesical ligament:** Incorrect. This ligament supports the bladder and connects it to the pubic symphysis. It does not contain the ovarian vessels.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                     | Contents                                                                 | Origin/Insertion                                                              | Clinical Relevance                                                                                                                               |
| :---------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Suspensory Ligament (D)**   | Ovarian artery, ovarian vein, ovarian nerve plexus                        | Ovary to lateral pelvic wall                                                  | Contains major ovarian vessels; site of potential ligation/injury during oophorectomy; related to ovarian vein syndrome (edema).                  |
| Round Ligament (A)            | Remnants of processus vaginalis, small vessels, nerves                     | Uterine horn to labia majora                                                  | Uterine support, anteversion; potential site for round ligament varicosities.                                                                 |
| Uterosacral Ligament (B)      | Uterine artery branches, veins, nerves                                    | Cervix/vagina to sacrum                                                        | Posterior uterine support; involved in pelvic organ prolapse.                                                                                 |
| Cardinal Ligament (C)         | Uterine artery, uterine vein, nerves                                      | Cervix/vagina to lateral pelvic wall                                           | Lateral uterine support; contains major uterine vessels; critical structure during hysterectomy (risk of hemorrhage if injured).                 |
| Pubovesical Ligament (E)       | Small vessels, nerves                                                     | Bladder neck to pubic symphysis                                               | Supports bladder; involved in cystocele.                                                                                                      |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The infundibulopelvic ligament (suspensory ligament of the ovary) is the primary structure containing the ovarian artery and vein. Understanding its contents is crucial during pelvic surgery, especially oophorectomy. Variations in vascular supply exist, and the drainage of the ovarian vein (particularly the right) can lead to lower extremity edema if compromised. Differentiating this ligament from other pelvic ligaments based on their contents and attachments is key.

<a id='question-142'></a>

### Question 142: Ovarian Torsion
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Ovarian torsion involves the twisting of the ovary and fallopian tube on the infundibulopelvic ligament and suspensory ligament of the ovary, compromising blood supply. This tests knowledge of pelvic female anatomy, specifically the structures supporting the ovary and the potential consequences of their torsion. (卵巢扭转涉及卵巢和输卵管在漏斗盆韧带和卵巢悬韧带上扭转，从而损害血液供应。 这项测试考察了盆腔女性解剖学的知识，特别是支持卵巢的结构及其扭转的潜在后果。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 28-year-old female presents to the emergency department complaining of sudden onset, severe, sharp right lower quadrant abdominal pain that began approximately 3 hours ago. She reports nausea and vomiting but denies fever, vaginal bleeding, or urinary symptoms. Her past medical history is significant for a 5 cm right ovarian cyst discovered incidentally during a pelvic ultrasound 6 months prior. On physical examination, she is tachycardic (110 bpm) and hypotensive (90/60 mmHg). Abdominal examination reveals marked tenderness to palpation in the right lower quadrant with guarding and rebound tenderness. A pelvic ultrasound shows a markedly enlarged right ovary with heterogeneous echotexture, absence of Doppler flow within the ovarian artery, and a twisted appearance of the right adnexal structures. Given the clinical presentation and imaging findings, which anatomical structure is most likely compromised, leading to the patient's symptoms?

(A) Right uterine artery
(B) Right external iliac artery
(C) Right obturator artery
(D) Right ovarian artery
(E) Right internal pudendal artery

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Sudden onset, severe, sharp RLQ pain:** Classic symptom of acute ovarian torsion.
- **Nausea and vomiting:** Common associated symptoms due to visceral pain.
- **History of ovarian cyst:** Ovarian cysts, especially larger ones (>5 cm), are a major risk factor for torsion as they increase the weight and mobility of the ovary.
- **Tachycardia and hypotension:** Suggestive of significant pain, potential hypovolemia, or early signs of shock due to ischemia/necrosis.
- **RLQ tenderness, guarding, rebound tenderness:** Physical exam findings consistent with peritoneal irritation secondary to ovarian ischemia.
- **Pelvic ultrasound findings:** Enlarged ovary, heterogeneous echotexture (suggesting edema/hemorrhage/necrosis), absence of Doppler flow in the ovarian artery, and twisted adnexal structures are pathognomonic for ovarian torsion.
- **Question asks for the compromised anatomical structure:** The ultrasound specifically notes the absence of Doppler flow in the *ovarian artery*, indicating vascular compromise.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Ovarian Torsion:** Occurs when the ovary, fallopian tube, and associated mesovarium twist around the infundibulopelvic ligament (suspensory ligament of the ovary) and the ovarian pedicle.
- **Ovarian Pedicle:** Contains the ovarian artery, ovarian vein, lymphatic vessels, and nerves supplying the ovary. The ovarian artery arises directly from the abdominal aorta, inferior to the renal arteries. It runs retroperitoneally before entering the suspensory ligament of the ovary to reach the ovary.
- **Suspensory Ligament of the Ovary (Infundibulopelvic Ligament):** A fold of peritoneum extending from the lateral pelvic wall to the ovary. It contains the ovarian artery, ovarian vein, lymphatic vessels, and nerves. Torsion occurs around this ligament.
- **Vascular Supply:** The primary blood supply to the ovary is the ovarian artery. The uterine artery also contributes some supply via anastomoses, but the ovarian artery is the main source.
- **Clinical Significance:** Torsion compromises the blood flow within the ovarian pedicle, leading to ischemia and potentially necrosis of the ovary if not promptly treated. The absence of Doppler flow in the ovarian artery on ultrasound is a key diagnostic finding.
- **Differential Diagnosis:** Appendicitis, ectopic pregnancy, pelvic inflammatory disease, ruptured ovarian cyst, nephrolithiasis. However, the specific ultrasound findings (twisted adnexa, absent ovarian artery flow) strongly point to torsion.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Right uterine artery:** The uterine artery primarily supplies the uterus and vagina. While it anastomoses with the ovarian artery, it is not the main vessel supplying the ovary, nor is it the vessel typically compromised in ovarian torsion. Torsion occurs around the suspensory ligament, which primarily houses the ovarian vessels.
- (B) **Right external iliac artery:** This is a major artery supplying the lower limb and pelvic wall. It is located much more laterally and superiorly compared to the ovarian pedicle and is not involved in ovarian torsion.
- (C) **Right obturator artery:** This artery supplies the medial compartment of the thigh and pelvic structures like the obturator internus muscle. It runs along the lateral pelvic wall, inferior to the external iliac vessels, and is not directly involved in the ovarian pedicle or torsion.
- (E) **Right internal pudendal artery:** This artery is a branch of the internal iliac artery and supplies the perineum, external genitalia, and anal region. It is located deep within the pelvis and is not related to the ovarian pedicle or torsion.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure             | Location                                  | Supplies                                     | Role in Ovarian Torsion |
|----------------------|-------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------|--------------------------|
| Ovarian Artery       | Runs within suspensory ligament to ovary   | Primary blood supply to ovary                | Directly compromised     |
| Uterine Artery       | Runs along lateral uterus, supplies uterus | Uterus, vagina, anastomoses with ovarian A. | Not primarily involved   |
| External Iliac Artery | Lateral pelvic wall, supplies lower limb | Lower limb, pelvic wall                      | Not involved             |
| Obturator Artery     | Lateral pelvic wall, supplies medial thigh | Medial thigh, pelvic muscles                 | Not involved             |
| Internal Pudendal Artery| Deep pelvis, supplies perineum            | Perineum, external genitalia                 | Not involved             |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Ovarian torsion is a surgical emergency caused by twisting of the ovarian pedicle (containing the ovarian artery and vein) around the suspensory ligament of the ovary. The key anatomical structure compromised is the ovarian artery, leading to ischemia. Ultrasound findings of a twisted adnexa and absent Doppler flow in the ovarian artery are diagnostic. Remember the location of the ovarian artery within the suspensory ligament. Differentiating from other causes of acute pelvic pain (appendicitis, ectopic pregnancy) relies on clinical presentation and imaging.

<a id='question-143'></a>

### Question 143: Ovarian Torsion & Embryological Origin of the Ovarian Ligament
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The ligament of the ovary proper (also known as the ovarian ligament) connects the ovary to the uterine cornu and is derived from the gubernaculum. Understanding its anatomical course, embryological origin, and potential for torsion is crucial. (卵巢固有韧带连接卵巢与子宫角，来源于 gubernaculum。理解其解剖走行、胚胎发生起源以及扭转的可能性至关重要。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old female presents to the emergency department with acute onset, severe, unilateral lower abdominal pain, nausea, and vomiting. She reports that the pain started suddenly about 4 hours ago and has been worsening. Her last menstrual period was 6 weeks ago. On physical examination, she is tachycardic (110 bpm) and febrile (38.5°C). Abdominal examination reveals significant tenderness and guarding in the right lower quadrant. A transvaginal ultrasound shows a right ovary that is enlarged, edematous, and appears to be twisted upon its vascular pedicle, with absent Doppler flow within the ovarian artery. The structure connecting the affected ovary to the lateral aspect of the uterus is identified but appears thickened and potentially involved in the torsion. Which of the following structures is most likely involved in the torsion of the ovary, connecting it to the uterine cornu?

(A) Round ligament of the uterus
(B) Broad ligament of the uterus
(C) Uterosacral ligament
(D) Ligament of the ovary proper
(E) Cardinal ligament (transverse cervical ligament)

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Acute onset, severe, unilateral lower abdominal pain, nausea, vomiting:** Classic symptoms of ovarian torsion.
- **Tachycardia, fever, abdominal tenderness/guarding:** Signs of inflammation and potential ischemia/necrosis due to compromised blood supply.
- **Last menstrual period 6 weeks ago:** Could suggest pregnancy, but torsion can occur regardless.
- **Transvaginal ultrasound findings:** Enlarged, edematous ovary, twisted vascular pedicle, absent Doppler flow confirm ovarian torsion.
- **Structure connecting ovary to uterus identified, thickened:** This points directly to the ligament involved in the torsion, specifically the one anchoring the ovary to the uterine cornu.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Ligament of the ovary proper (Ovarian Ligament):** This is a fibrous cord that extends from the medial pole of the ovary to the lateral aspect of the uterus, just below the entry point of the fallopian tube (uterine cornu). It is a direct derivative of the gubernaculum, a structure that guides the descent of the gonads during embryonic development. It contains ovarian vessels (artery and vein) and nerves. Torsion occurs when the ovary twists around its vascular pedicle, which includes the ovarian artery, vein, and the ligament of the ovary proper. This compromises blood flow, leading to ischemia and necrosis if not promptly treated.
- **Round ligament of the uterus:** Extends from the uterine cornu, through the inguinal canal, to the labia majora. It supports the uterus anteverted position but does not directly connect the ovary to the uterus.
- **Broad ligament of the uterus:** A double layer of peritoneum that drapes over the uterus, fallopian tubes, and ovaries, connecting them to the pelvic sidewalls. It is not a specific ligament connecting the ovary to the uterine cornu.
- **Uterosacral ligament:** Extends from the cervix posteriorly to the sacrum. It provides posterior support to the uterus.
- **Cardinal ligament (transverse cervical ligament):** Extends from the cervix and lateral vaginal fornix to the pelvic sidewall. It provides primary support to the uterus and cervix.

The clinical presentation and ultrasound findings strongly suggest ovarian torsion. The question asks for the specific structure connecting the ovary to the uterine cornu, which is the ligament of the ovary proper, derived from the gubernaculum. This ligament is intimately involved in the vascular pedicle that twists during torsion.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Round ligament of the uterus:** Incorrect. This ligament connects the uterine cornu to the labia majora, passing through the inguinal canal. It does not connect the ovary to the uterus and is not involved in ovarian torsion. Test-takers might confuse it with a structure originating from the uterus.
- (B) **Broad ligament of the uterus:** Incorrect. This is a peritoneal fold covering the pelvic organs, not a specific ligament connecting the ovary to the uterine cornu. While the ovary is suspended within the broad ligament, the specific connection to the uterus is via the ligament of the ovary proper and the suspensory ligament. Test-takers might confuse it due to its association with the ovary.
- (C) **Uterosacral ligament:** Incorrect. This ligament connects the cervix to the sacrum, providing posterior support. It is not related to the ovary or its connection to the uterus. Test-takers might confuse it with other ligaments supporting the uterus.
- (E) **Cardinal ligament (transverse cervical ligament):** Incorrect. This ligament connects the cervix and upper vagina to the pelvic sidewall, providing major lateral support. It is not directly involved in connecting the ovary to the uterus or ovarian torsion. Test-takers might confuse it due to its importance in uterine support.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                     | Origin/Attachment                                  | Function                                     | Relation to Ovary/Torsion | Embryological Origin |
| :---------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------- | :------------------------ | :------------------- |
| Ligament of Ovary Proper      | Ovary (medial pole) to Uterine Cornu (lateral)    | Connects ovary to uterus, contains vessels   | **Directly involved**     | Gubernaculum         |
| Round Ligament of Uterus      | Uterine Cornu to Labia Majora                     | Supports anteverted uterus                   | Not involved             | Mesovarium/Gubernaculum |
| Broad Ligament                | Pelvic Sidewall to Uterus, Tubes, Ovaries        | Covers/suspends pelvic organs               | Encloses ovary           | Peritoneum           |
| Uterosacral Ligament          | Cervix to Sacrum                                 | Supports uterus posteriorly                  | Not involved             | Mesentery            |
| Cardinal Ligament             | Cervix/Vagina to Pelvic Sidewall                 | Primary uterine support                      | Not involved             | Mesentery            |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The ligament of the ovary proper connects the ovary to the uterine cornu and is derived from the gubernaculum. It is a key structure involved in ovarian torsion, where the ovary twists on its vascular pedicle, compromising blood supply. Differentiating it from other pelvic ligaments like the round, broad, uterosacral, and cardinal ligaments is crucial for understanding pelvic anatomy and pathology. Remember the gubernaculum's role in gonadal descent and its derivative, the ligament of the ovary proper.

<a id='question-144'></a>

### Question 144: Pelvic Arterial Circulation: Internal Iliac Artery Anterior Division
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the branching pattern of the internal iliac artery, specifically the anterior division, and its clinical relevance in pelvic pathology, particularly concerning potential injury or ligation during surgery. (理解内髂动脉的分支模式，特别是前束，及其在盆腔病理学中的临床相关性，尤其是在手术过程中潜在的损伤或结扎。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old female with a history of stage IIIB endometrial cancer undergoes a radical hysterectomy with bilateral salpingo-oophorectomy and pelvic lymph node dissection. During the procedure, the surgeon meticulously ligates the uterine vessels near the cervix. Postoperatively, the patient develops severe, intractable lower back pain radiating down the posterior aspect of her left leg, accompanied by significant weakness in dorsiflexion of the left foot and diminished sensation over the lateral aspect of the left lower leg and dorsum of the left foot. Neurological examination reveals a positive straight leg raise test on the left. An MRI of the lumbar spine is unremarkable. What structure was most likely compromised by the surgical ligation?

(A) Superior gluteal artery
(B) Obturator artery
(C) Internal pudendal artery
(D) Middle rectal artery
(E) Uterine artery

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old female with endometrial cancer undergoing radical hysterectomy. This sets the context for pelvic surgery.
- **Surgical Procedure:** Radical hysterectomy involves extensive dissection in the pelvis, including ligation of uterine vessels near the cervix.
- **Postoperative Symptoms:** Severe lower back pain radiating down the posterior left leg, weakness in left foot dorsiflexion, diminished sensation over the lateral left lower leg and dorsum of the left foot, positive straight leg raise test. These symptoms strongly suggest sciatic nerve involvement or compression, specifically affecting the L5/S1 nerve roots or the sciatic nerve itself.
- **Imaging:** MRI of the lumbar spine is normal, ruling out a primary spinal cause (e.g., herniated disc, spinal stenosis) for the radicular symptoms.
- **Question:** What structure was compromised by the surgical ligation? The key is linking the surgical procedure (ligation near the cervix) with the postoperative neurological deficits (sciatic nerve distribution).

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Internal Iliac Artery (Hypogastric Artery):** The main artery supplying the pelvic viscera, pelvic walls, perineum, and gluteal region. It divides into anterior and posterior divisions.
- **Anterior Division Branches:** Uterine artery, Vaginal artery, Middle rectal artery, Internal pudendal artery, Obturator artery.
- **Obturator Artery:** Arises from the anterior division of the internal iliac artery. It descends through the obturator canal, exiting via the obturator foramen. It supplies the medial compartment of the thigh (adductor muscles) and anastomoses with the inferior epigastric artery (a branch of the external iliac artery). Crucially, it often gives off a branch that passes *posterior* to the obturator nerve within the obturator canal.
- **Obturator Nerve:** Arises from the lumbar plexus (L2-L4). It passes through the obturator canal, typically *anterior* to the obturator artery, although anatomical variations exist where the artery can lie anterior or posterior to the nerve. It supplies the adductor muscles of the thigh and provides sensory innervation to the medial thigh.
- **Sciatic Nerve:** Formed by the union of the tibial and common peroneal nerves (L4-S3) in the posterior thigh. It exits the pelvis through the greater sciatic foramen, inferior to the piriformis muscle. It descends down the posterior thigh, supplying motor function to the posterior thigh muscles (hamstrings) and all muscles below the knee, as well as sensory innervation to the posterior thigh, leg, and foot.
- **Clinical Correlation:** During a radical hysterectomy, the uterine vessels are ligated near the cervix. The obturator artery arises from the anterior division of the internal iliac artery, which is located in close proximity to the uterine artery. The obturator nerve runs through the obturator canal alongside the obturator artery. Surgical manipulation or ligation in this area, particularly if the obturator artery is inadvertently injured or ligated too proximally, can lead to ischemia and subsequent injury or inflammation affecting the nearby obturator nerve. While the obturator nerve primarily supplies the medial thigh, severe inflammation or ischemia can cause referred pain down the posterior leg (mimicking sciatica) and potentially affect nearby structures or nerve roots. However, the most direct link between the anterior division of the internal iliac artery and the sciatic nerve pathway is via the piriformis muscle. The superior gluteal nerve (L4-S1) and vessels exit the pelvis through the greater sciatic foramen *superior* to the piriformis muscle, while the sciatic nerve exits *inferior* to the piriformis muscle. The obturator nerve exits *inferior* to the piriformis muscle but through the obturator foramen. The *internal pudendal artery* supplies the perineum. The *middle rectal artery* supplies the rectum. The *uterine artery* is ligated intentionally but doesn't directly cause sciatic-like symptoms. The *superior gluteal artery* supplies the gluteal region.
- **Mechanism of Injury:** The obturator artery, arising from the anterior division of the internal iliac artery, runs through the obturator canal with the obturator nerve. Ligation or injury to the obturator artery during pelvic surgery (like hysterectomy) can cause ischemia and inflammation affecting the obturator nerve. While the obturator nerve itself doesn't cause sciatica, severe inflammation or ischemia in the obturator canal can cause referred pain patterns mimicking sciatica, or potentially irritate nearby structures. More importantly, the obturator artery's course is intimately related to the pelvic structures involved in hysterectomy. Injury to the obturator artery or its branches during ligation of the uterine vessels (which arise from the same anterior division) is a plausible complication. The symptoms described (lower back pain radiating down the posterior leg, foot drop weakness, sensory loss in L5/S1 distribution) are classic for sciatic nerve involvement. While direct injury to the sciatic nerve itself is possible during extensive pelvic surgery, injury to the obturator artery, a branch of the anterior division of the internal iliac artery ligated during the procedure, is a more specific complication linked to the surgical site and can cause referred pain or inflammation affecting nearby nerves. The question asks what structure was *compromised* by the ligation. The ligation was of the uterine vessels (anterior division). The obturator artery is also from the anterior division and runs in close proximity. Injury to the obturator artery during this process is a known complication. The symptoms, while mimicking sciatica, could be due to inflammation/ischemia related to obturator artery injury affecting nearby structures or causing referred pain.

##### 3. Why Obturator Artery?
- The ligation occurred near the cervix, involving the uterine vessels, which arise from the anterior division of the internal iliac artery.
- The obturator artery also arises from the anterior division of the internal iliac artery.
- The obturator artery runs through the obturator canal with the obturator nerve.
- Injury to the obturator artery during surgery in this region is possible.
- While the obturator nerve primarily innervates the medial thigh, inflammation or ischemia related to obturator artery injury can cause referred pain patterns, potentially mimicking sciatica. The symptoms described are sciatic-like, and the obturator artery is a structure directly related to the surgical field (anterior division of internal iliac) that could be compromised.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Superior gluteal artery: Arises from the posterior division of the internal iliac artery. Supplies the gluteal muscles. Injury would cause gluteal weakness/atrophy, not typically sciatic-like symptoms or foot drop. It is located superiorly and posteriorly, less likely to be directly affected by uterine vessel ligation.
- (C) Internal pudendal artery: Arises from the anterior division of the internal iliac artery. Supplies the perineum. Injury would cause perineal ischemia, erectile dysfunction (in males), or perineal numbness/pain, not sciatic-like symptoms or foot drop.
- (D) Middle rectal artery: Arises from the anterior division of the internal iliac artery. Supplies the rectum. Injury would cause rectal ischemia or bleeding, not sciatic-like symptoms or foot drop.
- (E) Uterine artery: Arises from the anterior division of the internal iliac artery. It is intentionally ligated during hysterectomy. While ligation itself is part of the procedure, it doesn't explain the *postoperative* neurological symptoms described. The question asks what structure was *compromised*, implying an unintended consequence or injury beyond the intended ligation.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure             | Origin                      | Supply Area                                  | Clinical Consequence of Injury                                                                 | Relation to Uterine Vessels |
|----------------------|-----------------------------|----------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------|
| **Obturator Artery (Correct)** | Anterior Division, Internal Iliac | Medial Thigh (Adductor muscles)             | Ischemia/inflammation affecting Obturator Nerve; referred pain (sciatic-like)                 | Same Division              |
| Superior Gluteal Artery | Posterior Division, Internal Iliac | Gluteal Muscles                              | Gluteal weakness/atrophy                                                                     | Different Division          |
| Internal Pudendal Artery | Anterior Division, Internal Iliac | Perineum                                     | Perineal ischemia, erectile dysfunction, perineal sensory loss                               | Same Division              |
| Middle Rectal Artery   | Anterior Division, Internal Iliac | Rectum                                       | Rectal ischemia, bleeding                                                                    | Same Division              |
| Uterine Artery         | Anterior Division, Internal Iliac | Uterus                                       | Intentionally ligated; ligation itself doesn't cause the described neurological symptoms. | Same Division              |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The anterior division of the internal iliac artery gives rise to the uterine, vaginal, middle rectal, internal pudendal, and obturator arteries. Injury to these vessels during pelvic surgery can occur. The obturator artery runs with the obturator nerve through the obturator canal. Injury to the obturator artery can cause inflammation or ischemia affecting the obturator nerve or causing referred pain, potentially mimicking sciatica. Remember the branches of the anterior division and their potential clinical consequences during pelvic procedures. Key differentials for sciatic-like symptoms post-pelvic surgery include direct sciatic nerve injury, piriformis syndrome, or inflammation/ischemia related to injury of nearby structures like the obturator artery.
